# gamelab

gamelab drives the real game with exact key timelines, records it, and measures what happened. It started as the way MapleHelper's combo timing was measured: which way she jumped and threw, and whether the game ignored a key. A regular attack locks out jumps and attacks for about 730 ms. Quick taps right after takeoff were missed about 6% of the time.

On top of that it hunts on its own: it finds her and the monsters on screen, places them on the map, plans routes and fights, and sweeps a map platform by platform.

## Layers

The code is organized like a self-driving car's stack. Each layer is a package, and a layer only imports the layers below it (`tests/test_architecture.py` checks this).

```
  behavior      what to do next: the hunter (the agent), fights, looting, tours
     |
     +-- attack       which monster, from where, and how                    [pure decisions]
     +-- control      decisions turned into keys: actions, one move, a route; the key player
     +-- motion       motion planning, A to B: physics, routes, chained key timelines   [pure]
     +-- localization where she, the monsters and the drops are on the map (mined map data)
     +-- perception   pixels to what's on screen (no map knowledge)

  ui            the live mini-map window (reads telemetry)
  sim           an offline game: the stack runs against it with no game and no keys
  analysis      measuring combos and her movement in recordings
  shared        paths, screen geometry, left and right (directions), telemetry, the game window, screen capture,
                gamelab's few pieces in C (native)
```

| Package | Does | Main pieces | May import |
|---|---|---|---|
| `perception` | Pixels to what's on screen, in half-size screen px | `Eyes` (the only door to pixels): `look` gives an `Observation` (her name tag, HP/MP, the ground under her, her minimap dot and other players', ropes, her EXP number and the box it's read from); `monsters`, `items`, `bar`, `refind` and `heading` (which way she faces, off her picture: `Heading`) answer questions about a look's frame. `MobDetector`, `LootDetector`, `NameTag`, `LieDetectorSpotter`; `exp_number` (her EXP number read, glyph by glyph); `frames` (the game as the screen shows it, for the continuous feed, never brought to the front) | shared |
| `localization` | Where things are on the map, in map units | `mapdata` (the map from the game's own data), `pose` (her position from the minimap dot, refined by ropes or her platform's end; the `Camera`), `Scene` (a look placed on the map), `Monster`/`MonsterTrack`/`Tracker` (monsters seen, told apart from look to look, and where they can be next), `Players` (other players, by their minimap dots), `Senses` (every look goes through it; its sweep searches the screen a slice a look), `Memory` (the monsters she has seen anywhere, kept across looks: Her memory of the monsters), `gains` (what each EXP gain was: the kills its size names, by kind; her EXP a minute off the number); the continuous feed: `tracking` (monsters followed frame by frame: ids, deaths and who killed them), `worldstate` (the feed's step, a frame at a time), `feed` (its child process, the stack's side of it, and `SyncFeed` for the simulator) | perception |
| `motion` | Getting from A to B | `body` (how the game moves her: the client's movement step by step, from its own Physics.img), `physics` (jumps, falls and walking, measured), `route` (every move between platforms; Dijkstra; the ways a trip may choose among: Route diversity), `flow` (several moves chained into one key timeline, hopping monsters) | localization |
| `attack` | Which monster, from where, how | `reach` (where a star hits, and what an attack from a jump can pick), `stance` (throw, jump-throw, jump back, step away, close in), `spots` (where to stand to shoot a platform), `danger` (how badly she can be touched at a place: the monsters seen and the spawn points, by game data) | perception (monster names), localization, motion |
| `control` | Keys out | `Actions` (her key plans: throws, walking, pick-up, potions, the chair), `Mover` (one move, closed loop), `Navigator` (a route: chains, glances, cut-offs, replans), `client` (plans to the key player), `player` (the key player itself), `alarm`, `lie_recorder` (the Lie Detector's cycle recorded), `rolling` (the game's last 5 minutes kept: The rolling recording) | perception, localization, motion |
| `behavior` | What to do next | `Hunter` (the agent: senses, actions, a looter, her vitals and health), `fight` (`clear`, `kill`, fighting on the way), `Looter` (sweeps, mop-ups, drops remembered), `Tour`, `probe` (the in-game probes of what a star hits, and their scorer), `PetCare` (her pet fed), `retreat` (on the Lie Detector, away to a safe place within 10 s) | all of the above |
| `ui` | The mini-map window | `MapView` (draws a state; pure), `State` (the latest record per topic), sources (live, replay, a planned route), `MapWindow` | localization, motion, attack |
| `sim` | An offline game | `World` (her body, monsters, kills, drops), `FakeEyes`, `FakePlayer` (the Lie Detector too: `alarm_at`), 124 scenarios on three maps, golden traces | everything but ui |
| `analysis` | Combo tests and her movement on recordings; how her routes are used | `track`, `scroll`, `analyze`, `calibration`, `recordings`, `physics_check` (her tracked movement against the physics models), `coverage` (each route move planned, taken and missed, and why the others lose: Route coverage), `respawn` (how soon a platform she empties has a monster again, how long drops lie, how full the platforms are, from the telemetry: Respawns and drop life) | perception, motion, control |

How a look becomes keys:

```
Eyes.look -> Observation -> Senses.look -> Scene ─┬─> attack.stance ─────────────> Actions ─┐
   (perception)            (localization)         └─> motion.route / flow -> Navigator / Mover ─┴─> client -> player -> the game
                                         after each look: the hunter's vitals (EXP gains, HP), her heading off the frame (Actions.see) and telemetry
```

The loops live in `behavior/fight.py` (a fight), `control/navigator.py` (a route) and `behavior/tour.py` (a sweep).

## How the keys get to the game

MapleStory runs as administrator, and Windows blocks key presses from normal programs into admin windows. So one small process, the **player** (`control/player.py`), runs elevated, and everything else runs normally and talks to it through files in `gamelab/lab/`. It reads plans (JSON) from `lab/inbox`, presses keys only while the game is in front, records the game window if asked, and writes results to `lab/outbox`. `control/client.py` hands it plans and waits for the results; `control/timeline.py` runs MapleHelper's own combo code on a virtual clock, so tests play exactly what the helper plays.

What keeps the player safe:
- **No code from plans:** it runs no code it receives; plans are data.
- **Any key, and clicks inside the game** (since 2026-10-23, at the user's request: the key bindings window, `\`, needs a click on Confirm to save a change): it presses any key `config.toml` can name (`player.ALL_KEYS`) and both mouse buttons (`lbutton`, `rbutton` events; `act`'s `click@X,Y` and `rclick@X,Y`), and moves the pointer only inside the game window. Until then it pressed only her bound keys, `\` and Esc, never Enter, and never clicked.
- **Game in front only:** it stops pressing keys if the game leaves the foreground.
- **Fixed at start:** it loads its code and key list once, when it starts, and loads as little of gamelab as it can (a test checks).
- **Stops itself:** it exits when `lab/STOP` appears or after 24 idle hours (3 until 2026-10-23: the user asked that it not be exited proactively, since starting it again needs their UAC click; not while it records a Lie Detector), and after a Lie Detector once you've cleared it (below).
- **Plans can be cut short:** when `lab/inbox/<plan id>.cancel` appears, it stops pressing and lets go of every key it holds. A plan it hasn't started yet can be withdrawn: it renames each plan as it takes it, so the client can tell. The navigator uses this when she's hit or off course mid-plan.
- **Plans can be queued:** a plan can say how long it lasts (`end_ms`), so the next one starts the moment it ends. Keys are still all released between plans.
- **Stops on the Lie Detector:** the player checks the game screen 4 times a second, idle or not. It recognizes the countdown window (yellow "LIE DETECTOR" title), the mouse mini-game's notice 10 s before it, the mouse mini-game ("The Lie Detector Mini-game: use the mouse to follow the fading figure"), the image quiz's warning 30 s before it, the image quiz ("Click all matching images"), and the Truth Booth that a failed test sends her to (the notice and the quiz since 2026-10-25: below). When one appears, it:
  1. stops all input within about 10 ms, mid-move included, and lets go of any keys and mouse buttons a plan held: from then on that's all that goes out, so none stays held;
  2. writes `lab/ALARM.json` first, then saves a screenshot (once, exiting mid-save left neither);
  3. pages whoever is at the PC with alert sounds and a Windows notification that doesn't take focus from the game, even when `ALARM.json` can't be written (the disk full: it had raised before the paging, and nobody was told);
  4. records the whole cycle: every frame it grabs until the Lie Detector has been off the screen for a minute (see The Lie Detector's recordings);
  5. stays up, locked: every plan is answered with the Lie Detector error, but for the two kinds below (her pet's food, her retreat's moves). Once you have run `python -m gamelab clear-alarm` and the Lie Detector has been off the screen for a minute (`lie_recorder.END_S`), it stops (its log says so, then "player stopped"), and plans are taken again only once you start it again (`player --elevate`, your UAC click), as when it shut down at the alarm. Until then it goes on watching: a new Lie Detector is paged and recorded as the first. Until 2026-10-24 it shut down at once, its watchdog with it, so nothing it saved showed the Lie Detector being answered. It doesn't take plans again by itself: then `clear-alarm`, an ordinary command any program can run, would be all that stood between the keys and a Lie Detector the spotter can't see (the figure tracking, below), and starting it again is the step only a person at the PC takes. So `clear-alarm` refuses while the recording is still going (exit code 3), and the alarm cleared anyway (its file deleted) is raised again, and paged, at the next sight of the Lie Detector. An `ALARM.json` that couldn't be written keeps it up and locked until it's stopped: nothing could then tell that a person cleared it.

  It never tries to answer the test, and never moves her by itself (it knows neither the map nor where she is). While it's up it sends step 1's letting go, and only two kinds of plan, each only as its kind says (`player.alarm_refusal`; since 2026-10-24, the user, after a Lie Detector failed over Chrome Remote Desktop's lag and the alarm then kept her pet from being fed: "just stop any in-progress hunt when this happens, don't block pet feeding", and "On a lie detector you should move to a safe place, within 10s"). A plan says its kind (`client.send(kind=...)`, `alarm.PLAN_KINDS`):
  - **`pet_food`**: one tap of her pet food key (`config.toml` `pet_food`, U) and nothing else, the whole plan over within 200 ms of its start (`player.TAP_MAX_MS`: its key down and up, its `end_ms`; a food planned later, or lasting longer, would hold the player meanwhile, her retreat's moves and its stop once the alarm is cleared waiting behind it): no other key, no mouse, no resize, no recording; 3 in any minute at most (`alarm.PET_FOOD_PER_MIN`; a food is due every 75 s or so at the most often). The pet food key with anything else is refused.
  - **`retreat`**: her moves alone (her arrows, jump, up and down: walking, jumping, climbing, dropping), every key it presses let go by its end, no mouse; for 12 s after the sighting (`alarm.RETREAT_WINDOW_S`; when it was seen is the alarm Event's, `player.Alarm.at`), a plan still running then cut there and its keys let go, and none taken after. Either kind starts within 200 ms (its `lead_ms`; a plan's lead was bounded only by the frames it records). Who sends it, and why it's 12 s: On the Lie Detector, below.

  Neither is ever brought to the front (whoever is at the PC is answering the test), each is logged ("Lie Detector alarm up: her pet's food ...", "... her retreat's plan ...", "... refused ..."), and any other plan is refused as before, by the client already (exit code 3). The two kinds get past the client's check of `ALARM.json` (the player decides), so the player holds them to these rules with the file up too when its own watchdog hasn't seen the Lie Detector (`watch --lie-detector` raised it), a retreat's 12 s counted from the file's `at` ("Lie Detector alarm up (its file's; this player hasn't seen it): ..."); until a review on 2026-10-24 such a plan played as any, the game brought to the front. A plan running when the Lie Detector is seen stops at once whatever its kind; one refused raises `client.LieDetectorAlarm` with why, also when the alarm file isn't written yet. The alarm, its recording, `clear-alarm` and the player's stop and start again are as they were. Until you run `clear-alarm`, every command that sends keys or brings the game to the front refuses to run (exit code 3), including starting the player again and `probe run` (`spot`, `minimap`, `sim`, `map --offline`, `probe list`, `probe score` and the recording tools still run). A player started again takes no plan before its watchdog's first look at the game, so a Lie Detector still on screen (the Truth Booth) is seen before any key, and it stops if its watchdog does (nothing would see one). A plan waiting on the game's window (a resize, the game brought to the front) stops there when the Lie Detector is seen meanwhile, before the game is brought forward: those waits weren't watched, the keys' own are. When Claude is driving gamelab, it also pushes the alert to your phone.

  On 2026-10-18 the mini-game form wasn't known yet. It went unanswered, and she was sent to the Truth Booth (entry count 1). It has been recognized since then.

  **The notice and the image quiz (since 2026-10-25).** The two mini-games of 23:51 and 00:55 that night were each announced 10 s ahead by a box at the bottom right ("The mini-game will start soon. Go to a safe place before the game begins."), and at 00:28 a new test came: a see-through box, "Question in 30 seconds / Answer within 10 seconds", counting down, then "Click all matching images" with three pictures and a Confirm button, 10 s to answer. The player alarmed only at the board, so the hunt attacked through both notices, and the quiz went by unseen, the hunt going on (the user answered all three). At 1920x1080 the board's template had caught the notice, just (0.80 against 0.8: the 2026-10-19 and 21 alarms were notices); at the game's own 1366x768 it scored 0.75. Each of the three now has its own half-size template (`perception.lie_detector.HALF_SIZE`: `mini-game notice`, `quiz warning`, `quiz`), cut from the rolling recorder's frames of that night. Replayed through the spotter, every saved frame of 2026-10-19 to 25 (12,548; `D:/MapleHelper-scratch/report-1025`): the alarm comes at the first frame of each notice (23:51:19.0, 00:55:00.6) and of the quiz's warning (00:27:58.0), 10 s and 30 s before the test, and on no frame but a Lie Detector's. Each is told for what it is (`ALARM.json`'s `form`, the log's "LIE DETECTOR seen (quiz warning)", the page: "MapleStory image quiz in 30 s"), and everything else is as for any form: input stopped, her retreat, the recording, the part before it. Not known: whether the quiz's warning always gives 30 s and the quiz always 10 (the template is its second line, "Answer within 10 seconds"), whether other quizzes have another title, how the see-through box reads over a bright map (both were seen only on Tree Dungeon), and what an unanswered quiz does.

## On the Lie Detector: the hunt stops, she retreats, her pet is still fed (from 2026-10-24)

**The hunt stops at once.** From the sighting the player refuses the hunt's plans: the one running is cut (its keys let go within about 10 ms) and its result raises `client.LieDetectorAlarm`; a hunt that is only looking for a while (waiting for respawns, standing on a spot, resting on the chair) stops at its next look, as every look of the hunter (`Hunter.look`), every pass of a navigator's trip and her pet care in a hunt (`Hunter.feed_pet_if_due`) check the alarm first. `hunt kill`, `hunt clear`, `nav`, `tour`, `tour --loop` and `probe run` then retreat (below), and stop with the Lie Detector's message and exit code 3, as before; `combo`, `lock`, `act`, `do` and the other commands that only press keys stop as before, with no retreat.

**Her retreat** (`behavior/retreat.py`, `retreat.on_alarm` around each of those commands' hunt): the hunt that was running moves her to a safe place within 10 s of the sighting (`alarm.RETREAT_S`), because it has the map, the danger model and the navigator; with no hunt running nothing moves her, and the player only raises the alarm and records. Live on 2026-10-24 and 25, seen at the board, she was on platform 25 at 3.4 s and 3.0 s; seen at the mini-game's notice or the quiz's warning (since 2026-10-25), her 10 s are over before the test begins. Her retreat counts from the alarm's `at` (`ALARM.json`, `time.time()` at the sighting; added for it):
- **Where to** (`retreat.choose`): of the safe places she gets to in time, by the route planner's time stretched for her looks and stops on the way (`trip_s`: 1.5 times it and 0.6 s), the nearest safe platform (`MapModel.safe`) or place the danger model rates safe enough (`attack.danger`: 1% of her HP at most, `SAFE_DANGER`, with every spawn point's monster at its point and with the look's monsters; on a map where no kind counts, as on the first, only a safe platform), if it's near (`NEAR_S`, 6 s by `trip_s`); else the quickest to get to of those and the ropes as safe (positioning's rope model, `attack.ropes`: from any platform she gets to, at its safest height; she regains no HP on a rope, so only when no safe platform is near); with none in time, the safest place she gets to, or where she is if that's it. On a safe place already, she stays, a rope as safe too (`retreat.safe_rope`: her dot still over three looks by a rope, its danger as a rope's in `choose`), where the hunt waits out monsters (rule 9); off a rope that isn't, at once. Until a review on 2026-10-24 she always got off a rope first, after the navigator's 2 s look until she stood still: in the simulator, off the rope at -8 (its danger 0) and across the map, 9.2 s after the sighting.
- **How**: her moves alone, as the navigator plans them avoiding monsters (`navigator.AVOID`, never a fight; the quickest way; a monster in the way of a move waited out where she can stand, or gone round, as on any trip: a hurry that did neither, tried first, took her through 18% more touches on the floor in the simulator for no more of her on a safe place at 10 s, review 2026-10-24), every plan of kind `retreat` (`Actions.moves_only`: her arrows, jump, up and down; no attack, pick-up or pet food, and no mouse: the mover's nudge of the pointer, for a tooltip over the minimap, sends nothing), each go given its trip's time and 1.5 s, and another from where she is if a touch knocked her back or a rope was missed (3 at most, `TRIES`) while the 10 s have time for one. No potion or pet food on the way: her pet's care stops with the hunt (the babysitter goes on: below).
- **What it says**: "LIE DETECTOR: retreat to platform 25 (a safe platform; 1.5 s by route, danger 0.000), 0.8 s after it was seen; her moves only, no mouse", then "LIE DETECTOR: retreat over (arrived): on platform 25 at x 260, 2.8 s after it was seen" (or how it stopped: "timeout", "not on the rope", the player's refusal past 12 s), and telemetry `event`s of kind `retreat`.
- **In the simulator** (`sim.fakes.FakePlayer.alarm_at`: the player sees it then, refusing as the real one does, `player.alarm_refusal` itself; scenarios `loop_tree_lie_detector`, `_28` and `_23`; `tests/test_retreat.py`): the hunt loop on Tree Dungeon, the Lie Detector seen at 40, 75, 110 or 150 s, seeds 1-12. Shooting 23 from 24, as live: on a safe place 10 s after the sighting in 47 of 48 runs (onto 25, 2.3 s after it at the median, 4.7 at p90). Hunting the whole map, mostly its floor (33) among its Green Mushrooms: 39 of 47 (83%), most onto the rope at 146 from 32 or onto 28 (no monster reaches it, though it isn't `MapModel.safe`), the rest knocked back on the way by the Greens on 32 and 30 (28 is the planner's 4.3 s from the floor, which took 5-12 s among them: past `NEAR_S`, so the rope comes first). In every run nothing but her moves went out after the sighting, and a retreat's plan past 12 s was refused (`D:/MapleHelper-scratch/ldpet/sweep.py`, after the review's fixes of 2026-10-24).
- **With the feed and the attack choice, and grinding** (work/integrate-3 rebased onto it, 2026-10-25): `tour --loop --grind` runs inside `on_alarm` as `tour --loop` does, and grinding (`behavior.grind.Grind`, a `Hunt`) stops at the alarm as the hunt loop does. With the feed, the default, the hunt notices it at its next look or pass as before (her looks still go through `Hunter.look`, her trips' passes through `Navigator._pass`); her retreat's looks come from the feed as any trip's, and its reactions cut a retreat's chain for a monster coming into its way with `client.cancel` (no key plan: the player takes it with the alarm up) before the next chain of kind `retreat`. Her retreat's actions (`moves_only`) have no Lucky Seven key and throw nothing, so the attack choice has nothing to decide there. Scenarios `feed_loop_tree_lie_detector` (as `tour --loop` runs by default from 28, the Lie Detector seen at 110 s: the feed, the attack choice, Lucky Seven costing MP, group 0's walls stopping her) and `loop_tree_lie_detector_grind` (as `tour --loop --grind` does, from 23, at 75 s): of seeds 1-12, 11 and 12 are on a safe place 10 s after the sighting with the monsters' bars read empty as before 2026-10-25 (Perception: Monster HP bars), 12 and 12 with them read; the retreat began within 0.22 s of it on every seed of the first, and within 0.59 s on 11 of the second (on seed 11 she was resting on 25, a safe platform, and the rest's next look came 5.0 s on: she stayed). In the default world, with only her group's walls, a touch knocked her out of the map before the sighting on 2 and 3 of the seeds (a simulator effect, as `loop_tree_run`'s). `tests/test_retreat.py`'s sweep from the floor's crowd (seeds 1-8, seen at 40, 75, 110 and 150 s) now runs with the feed, as `tour --loop` does by default: 24 of 29 on a safe place at 10 s (27 of 32 at master's head without it). **Without the feed** (`--no-feed`, and the simulator's default) the hunt loop sweeps the screen a slice a look (`hunt.SWEEP`, Her memory of the monsters), and there it was 15 of 32 (37 of 62 over seeds 1-16), against 28 of 29 with `hunt.SWEEP` off: the retreat is the same (the sweep is off by then), but the hunt before it had her on the floor more often at the sighting (38 of 61 runs against 30 of 61) and hit just before it more often (14 against 8), and from the same places her retreat fared worse (the floor and 32: 20 of 38 against 23 of 30). Not fixed; the test said so (a strict xfail) until her jumps' attacks were weighed and her jump throws turned in the air (2026-10-25, Attacks from her jumps): since then 26 of 32 without the feed, 28 of 32 with it (none out of the map), 32 of 32 shooting 23 from 24, the hunt before the sighting gone otherwise; with the review's fixes (her body clear of a monster above over a jump in place, a jump forward's landing however it goes, the ground's reach capped 60 above her again), 24 of 31 without the feed, 28 of 29 with it (three runs out of the map before the sighting), 32 of 32 shooting 23 from 24; with the ground's reach above 60 on again by the user's choice (`reach.UP_BY_SEARCH`), 25 of 32 without the feed, 28 of 29 with it (three out), 30 of 32 shooting 23 from 24; the sweep's cost isn't measured apart again. The feed leaves the sweep out of the looks it serves, so live it applies only while the feed rests or is gone.
- **Not done**: the retreat isn't tried in game; it doesn't know her HP (a retreat at 10% HP goes as one at 90%); its numbers (`SLACK`, `NEAR_S`, `SAFE_DANGER`) are the simulator's; a hunt standing still on looks that fail their checks (`LookLost`: Capture checks) doesn't notice the alarm until they pass, by when the 10 s are usually over (then "too late": she stays). When the alarm is noticed from a look rather than a plan, up to a pass's time (about 0.4 s) has gone by.

**Her pet is still fed.** The pet babysitter (`pet --every 1`) goes on through the alarm: its food (`Actions.feed_pet`, kind `pet_food`) by the fullness it follows in time (`behavior/pet.py`), never a read of her Character Info until the alarm is cleared (the read double-clicks her, and you may be answering the test with the mouse); a pet never read yet gets a food once 2 h have passed since the last, as when the panel can't be read. A food the player refuses (more than 3 a minute) or doesn't answer (the player stopped) is said and tried again at the next follow, where it used to end the command. Once you've cleared the alarm, the player stops as before (a minute after the Lie Detector's last sight), and the babysitter's foods wait for it to be started again.

**To put it live** (the player loads its code once: the running one keeps the old rules until it's started again): with the Lie Detector answered and off the screen for a minute, `python -m gamelab clear-alarm`; the running player stops by itself a minute after the Lie Detector's last sight (its log ends "player stopped"; `python -m gamelab stop` if not); then `python -m gamelab player --elevate` (your UAC click). Its log's first line says "with it seen: a tap of 'u' for her pet, 3 a minute, and her retreat's moves for 12 s; nothing else". A `pet --every 1` already running keeps the code it started with (it would end at the next Lie Detector): start it again too (no UAC).

**To check in game**, at the next Lie Detector (explore only: no attacks; a hunt running only when attack tests are allowed again): `pet --every 1` running, its foods go on ("pet fullness: about N"; the player's log "Lie Detector alarm up: her pet's food"), and no Character Info opens; with a hunt running, it stops at once, she moves to a safe place within 10 s by its log and the minimap, and nothing presses after 12 s; nothing moves the mouse while you answer. At the mini-game's notice or the quiz's warning (a player started since 2026-10-25: its log's "LIE DETECTOR seen (mini-game notice)" or "(quiz warning)"), all that within a second of the box showing, and she's on her safe place before the board or the quiz; `ALARM.json` says the `form`, the page names it, and the recording's folder has `before/`.

## The Lie Detector's recordings

Since 2026-10-24 the player's watchdog records each Lie Detector's whole cycle (`control/lie_recorder.py`): the user asked for it, as the player's logs stopped at the alarm and none showed them answering it. Only the player records (`watch --lie-detector` raises the alarm and pages, as before, and records nothing). One folder a cycle, `lab/lie_detector/<YYYYmmdd-HHMMSS>/`, named for when it was first seen:
- **`00000.jpg` ...**: every frame the watchdog grabs, 4 a second, from the first sight to the end: the game's client area, as it grabs it to check it. Nothing more: nothing of the mouse or the keyboard is recorded, and the watchdog never touches the game. JPEG at quality 75 with full-resolution colour: 297 KB a 1366×768 game frame, 197 KB a Lie Detector screen, 4.9 ms to encode, on a thread of its own. Measured on the three real 1366×768 frames in `tests/fixtures/native` and the six Lie Detector screens shrunk to 1366×768 (`D:/MapleHelper-scratch/ld/jpeg_measure2.py`): with the usual half-resolution colour (4:2:0) the countdown's thin yellow title lost its colour, the spotter scoring the decoded frame 0.36-0.41 of its threshold at every quality from 60 to 90, against 1.11 at full colour and 1.22 on the PNG; every other form scored as on the PNG, and the mini-game notice's small text and the HUD's read as on it. Quality 85 is 434 KB a frame; 65 was as legible at 238 KB, but rang round the letters. A PNG is 1,307 KB. At 1920×1080, the other size a plan may set (`window.CLIENT_SIZES`: the game's 1366×768 view stretched to it), a frame is half as big again: 447 KB a game frame (the three stretched), 335 KB a Lie Detector screen (126-431, the six as saved; `D:/MapleHelper-scratch/ld/finish/scripts/jpeg_sizes.py`). The sizes below are 1366×768's. `spot --image` on a recorded frame sees what the watchdog saw.
- **`index.jsonl`**: a line a grab: `t` (s since the first sight) and the frame's number `i`, its `file`, the spotter's `scores` for each form (match / threshold, 1 = seen) and `seen`; or, with the game window gone or minimized, `game` ("no window", "minimized") and no frame. Such a grab doesn't count as the Lie Detector gone: it may have been up behind. With another window over some of the game (`window.shows`: its middle and near each corner), the grab is still checked for the Lie Detector, as it always was (it can show round that window; a picture of it shown over the game, its screenshot in a viewer, is taken for it too), but its picture isn't saved and it doesn't count as gone either: `game` "covered", with the `scores` and `seen` of what was on top. A terminal opened over the game had gone to disk as frames of it, and a minute of it ended the cycle as answered. (Minimized, the game's client area isn't 0×0 but 432×54, so the watchdog now asks Windows whether it's minimized, where it grabbed whatever was at that spot.) A frame that couldn't be written has `error`; when the disk is too far behind to take one, it's dropped, and the next line's `dropped_before` says how many.
- **`summary.json`**, when it ends: `first_seen`, `last_seen` and `seen_s`; `duration_s`; `frames`, their bytes and the `grabs`; `not_showing` (by why); `forms` (each form seen, when it first and last showed and in how many frames); `truth_booth` and `outcome` (as far as the picture tells: "answered" if it went away without the Truth Booth showing, "sent to the Truth Booth", or "unknown" if it was still up when the recording ended); `end` ("gone", "cap", or "stopped": the player stopped); the alarm's screenshot; the `window`'s size; `write_errors` and `dropped`.

**YOLO shadow.** During a cycle started by the template spotter, the player also runs the local YOLO11n checkpoint (`assets/lie_detector_patterns.pt`, GPU 0 when CUDA is available, CPU otherwise) on the game's frames, on a thread of its own (`ShadowWorker`, `control/player.py`), never in the watchdog's looks. Its first check used to run in the look of the first sighting, and loaded torch, ultralytics and the model there: 5.0 s for the import and 3.9 s for the model and a first check on an idle PC, about 16 s live. The 21:02 recording of 2026-10-24 has frame 0, then nothing until 16.1 s, by when the Truth Booth showed: the whole mouse mini-game was lost, and the alarm waited as long. Now the player loads it when it starts and checks a blank frame once (its log: "the YOLO shadow is ready: loaded and a first check in N s, off the watch"; one that can't be loaded is turned off, the Lie Detector watched and recorded as ever). The watchdog hands it each frame of a cycle and grabs on; when it's free it checks the newest one handed it (4 a second at most; the others go unchecked). Its check comes back late, as a line of its own in the index after its frame's: the frame's `t` and `i`, its `shadow` score (the patterns found / 4), `seen`, `late` (s after the grab) and `suggestions`: every detected pattern's `box`, pointer-location `center` and `confidence`, in original frame pixels. These are detections, not a claim that YOLO knows the fading target or correct answer. Nothing moves the pointer or sends a key. Four or more detections at confidence 0.5 mark `yolo patterns` seen at that frame, and keep the cycle going through active pattern play after the notice disappears, as a sight of the Lie Detector there would: the minute without it starts after that frame, an alarm cleared since that frame is raised again, and the cycle's end waits for the shadow's check of a frame of that minute, 30 s at most (`SHADOW_WAIT_S`: a check that hangs holds up neither the end nor the player's stop). YOLO alone cannot start a cycle or an alarm. `summary.json`'s `shadow` counts its `checks` and `suggestions`, says how late the latest came back (`late_max_s`) and how many were `lost` (the disk too far behind). A YOLO failure is logged and leaves the template alarm and frame recording working. In `tests/test_lie_yolo.py` a shadow that takes 10 s a check leaves the frames coming 4 a second. To check at the next Lie Detector: frames 4 a second from 0 s, with no gap after the first but the paging's 1.2 s, and the shadow's lines a fraction of a second `late`.

**When it ends.** Once the game has shown no Lie Detector for a minute (`END_S`), the same minute the player waits, its alarm cleared, before it stops. The template spotter knows the mouse mini-game's notice; the YOLO shadow now recognizes active pattern boards too (its sight counted at the frame it checked, however late it comes back). The minute still has to outlast the count and tracking when neither sees a known form, the moment between the countdown and Violettas' windows, and a frame or two missed in a flicker. The first full recordings will show whether that tail needs changing. At the latest, recording stops 10 minutes after the first sight (`CAP_S`): the longest cycle seen is a test left unanswered and the Truth Booth it sends her to, then the minute, about 8 minutes. A cycle that runs on past it goes on unrecorded, the player locked, and no second recording starts until it's over.

**Off the alarm's path.** The watchdog sets the alarm Event first, then writes `ALARM.json` and its screenshot and pages, as before, and only then starts the recording, with the frame it saw. The paging's beeps take about 1.2 s, so the second frame comes that much after the first. The frames are written on a thread of their own behind a queue of 16 grabs (4 s), and old recordings deleted on another: a slow or full disk never holds up a look, nor does the YOLO shadow, on a thread of its own too. A frame that can't be written is logged once and counted, and the recording and the watching go on; nothing that goes wrong in a look, the recording or the log stops the watchdog (each is logged the first time and every 1,000th). The watchdog now looks 4 times a second from the start of each look. It used to wait 0.25 s after each, which with the look's own 70 ms made 3.10 a second on a 1366×768 frame (`D:/MapleHelper-scratch/ld/rate_measure.py`), 4.00 now; recording a Lie Detector screen, 4.00 a second with nothing dropped (`rate_recording.py`). That's about a third more of the check's time: 0.26 of a core at 65 ms a check, where it was 0.20.

**Disk.** When a recording starts, the oldest others are deleted until they take at most 10 GB (`KEEP_BYTES`), never the one being written. A cycle answered in about 2.5 minutes (the test's windows, then the minute's tail) is some 600 frames, about 175 MB at 290 KB a frame, so the 10 GB hold about 58 of those, 18 that went to the Truth Booth (8 minutes, 560 MB) or 14 at the cap (700 MB); at 1920×1080, about 260 MB a cycle answered, 39 of them. The Lie Detector came about an hour apart at the most often (00:47, 01:58 and 02:54 on 2026-10-19): that's two days of play without a break, weeks of evenings, where the telemetry's 2 GB keep 4 days of `watch`. `lab-usage` counts them, with what the next would delete, and `clear-alarm` refuses while the recording is still going, and says how the last one ended. The player's log has a line when one starts and when it ends (its frames, how long, the forms, the outcome). With the rolling recorder running, each cycle's folder also has the 5 minutes before it (`before/`, about 650 MB at 1366×768: The rolling recording), and they count: about 0.8 GB a cycle answered, 12 of them in the 10 GB. The recorder prunes the folder itself each time it writes a part, to the same 10 GB with the parts counted and the cycle under way spared (`Recorder._prune_lie`), as the player counts them only from that day's code on (`lie_recorder.size_of` counts a recording's folders since; before, its files only): an older player, elevated and restarted only with the user's click, would hold some 58 cycles of 0.8 GB, 37 GB, with 32 GB free on D: that evening.

**To check in game**, when the next Lie Detector comes (explore-only first: the player only watches it):
- Within two seconds of the alarm (after the paging's beeps), `lab/lie_detector/<time>/` appears and the player's log says "LIE DETECTOR seen", then "recording the Lie Detector"; the player keeps running, where it used to shut down.
- Answer it. A minute after it's gone, the log says "Lie Detector recording ended (gone): N frames over S s". `summary.json` should have about 4 frames a second, the forms you saw with their times, `outcome` "answered" (or the Truth Booth), `end` "gone" and `window` [1366, 768], and the frames should show you answering it. The mini-game notice's last `seen` in the index and the frames after it say how long the tracking took: `END_S` should outlast it with room (if the tracking ended within seconds of the recording, `END_S` goes up).
- `clear-alarm` before that minute is out refuses (exit code 3), saying the recording is still going; after it, it clears the alarm and says how the recording ended, and the player's log says "... the player stops", then "player stopped". Start it again (`player --elevate`); then `hunt look`, or `act 100` (no keys), runs. With a window over the game meanwhile (the terminal), the index has `"game": "covered"` lines and no frames for them, and the minute starts once the game shows again.
- `spot --image` on its first frame should say it's on screen; the alarm's screenshot goes with the others in `tests/fixtures/lie_detector`.

## The rolling recording

Since 2026-10-24 a recorder keeps the game's last 5 minutes, 10 frames a second, so that what led up to a moment can be saved after it (`control/rolling.py`). The user asked for it at 21:50 that evening: something had popped up in the game wanting an answer within 10 s, and nothing recorded it (the player's Lie Detector check knew only the countdown, the mouse mini-game and the Truth Booth, and records once it has seen one of them). It was most likely the image quiz, which the player knows since 2026-10-25, with its warning and the mini-game's notice: their alarms are saved as any (below). `python -m gamelab recorder` runs it, at below-normal priority, all the time, hunts too: the babysitter keeps it running. It reads the screen only: no keys, no clicks, and it never brings the game forward.

- **Only the game.** A frame is kept only when the screen shows the game's client area: its window there and not minimized, wholly on the monitors, in front, and no other program's window over any of it, asked before each grab and again after it, with the window where it was. Over it is asked twice: at `framecheck.COVER_GRID`'s 7×5 points as a look asks (`window.shows`), then up the z-order (`window.over`): every window above the game that's shown (visible, not minimized, not cloaked: a window on another virtual desktop isn't) and whose box as drawn (DWM's, without Windows 10's invisible borders) meets the client area. The points alone missed a window over the client's left 8 px, a 200×150 box between them and a 300×100 notification (the review's, on the real client area), and an overlay clicks pass through isn't found at a point at all. The game's own windows are left out. Its log names the window over the game (its class and box, not its title), so a window that's always there and keeps the recording off shows who it is. Otherwise the moment is a gap, and why ("no window", "minimized", "off the screen", "not in front", "covered", "moved", "capture failed"): nothing of another window is kept. Any client size is recorded.
- **In memory, losslessly** (since 2026-10-25, the user asked). A frame's BGR pixels are kept zstd-compressed at level 1 (`pack`: OpenCV drops the alpha, Python 3.14's `compression.zstd` packs; zlib on an older Python), exactly as grabbed. On six 1366×768 frames saved losslessly (three scenes, a calibration shot, two Lie Detector boards; `D:/MapleHelper-scratch` `lossless.py`, `ringbench.py`): 757 KB a frame (381-1163) packed in 9.5 ms from the grabbed BGRA; 3,000 of them, 5 minutes, 2.2 GB (3.4 at the busiest). Not PNG: as big, and 55 ms a frame at its fastest; WebP lossless 502 KB in 667 ms. They take at most 4 GB (`MAX_BYTES`, the PC has 32): past it the oldest go before they're 5 minutes old (at 1920×1080, twice the pixels, about 4 minutes at the busiest), counted in the status as `trimmed`. A part is encoded as it's written, on 4 threads (`ENCODERS`): JPEG at the Lie Detector recordings' settings (quality 75, full-resolution colour, so a frame of either reads alike: `spot --image` sees on one what the watchdog would have) for `before/` and `save-recent`, PNG for each alarm's clip (below): 5 minutes as JPEG in about 10 s (36 s of CPU, 741 MB), a clip's 70 s as PNG in about 14 s (44 s of CPU, 556 MB). Until then frames were kept as JPEG at those settings: on the 85 distinct saved 1366×768 game frames (`D:/MapleHelper-scratch/rolling/jpeg_measure2.py`) 221 KB a frame (47-307), 33.0 dB, 4.0 ms to encode, 650 MB for 5 minutes (a 712 MB process), at most 1 GB. In memory and not in a ring of files on D: because 10 frames a second are 2.2 MB/s, about 190 GB a day written, for frames almost never kept.
- **Captured with mss, not DXGI.** Windows limits the duplications of a monitor: with `watch` and `pet` running, two more opened and a third failed (DuplicateOutput 0x887A0022, DXGI_ERROR_NOT_CURRENTLY_AVAILABLE; `capture_bench.py`, 2026-10-24). A recorder holding one all the time would leave a hunt's looks and its feed one fewer, and one that can't open falls back to mss for `capture.RETRY_S` (10 s of 17 ms looks instead of 2 ms). mss holds no duplication (the player's watchdog grabs with it too). On the game's box, 5 s of 10 grabs a second each, nothing kept: DXGI 2.6-4.9 ms a grab (median) and 1.9-4.7 ms of CPU; mss 14-25 ms (it waits for the compositor's next frame; p90 25-35) and 5.3-6.6 ms of CPU, alone or with two others grabbing at once. (Since 2026-10-25 the commands hold one duplication between them, the perception service's, where `watch`, `pet` and a hunt held one each: the recorder stays on mss.)
- **Its cost** (`loop_bench.py`, the PC 66% busy with other work): a frame takes 22-32 ms from the first look at the window to its JPEG (median; mss's wait the most of it), and 12.7-14.3 ms of CPU, everything counted (the grab 6.7, the JPEG 4-6, the two looks at the window 0.5, measured before `window.over`; its walk up the z-order adds 0.1 ms a look, 0.66 in the review's run, 39 windows above the game): 0.13-0.14 of a core, at below-normal priority. Kept losslessly since 2026-10-25, a frame's packing takes 9.5 ms against the JPEG's 4-6: about 0.17 of a core, not measured in the live loop yet. While the game isn't showing it only looks at the window (0.02 ms a moment when the game isn't in front).
- **Saved on demand.** `save-recent [--seconds N] [--name NAME]` asks the running recorder through its inbox (`lab/recorder/inbox/<id>.json`; it looks every moment, renames the request `.taken` and answers in `lab/recorder/outbox/<id>.json`) to save its last N s (300 by default: all it keeps) to `lab/recent/<YYYYmmdd-HHMMSS>[-name]/`, and prints the folder last. The frames are those the ring held when it took the request, written on a thread of their own (5 minutes in about 10 s, encoded to JPEG as they're written; nothing waits for them). With no recorder running (its status file, `lab/recorder/status.json`, missing, saying it stopped, or older than 30 s), or none taking the request within 3 s (withdrawn then), it says so, exit code 1. When one is saved, the oldest others go until they take 5 GB (`RECENT_KEEP_BYTES`, about 8 of 5 minutes).
- **Saved on a Lie Detector alarm.** At an `ALARM.json` with a time it hasn't seen (looked for every 0.5 s), what it holds then, and what comes in the alarm's first 5 s (`BEFORE_TAIL_S`: the player writes the alarm, pages for about 1.2 s, then starts its recording), goes into that recording's folder as `before/`: the 5 minutes before the Lie Detector and its first seconds again, 10 a second, where the player's own frames are 4 a second from the first sight. It's written a minute after the alarm, with its clip (below), once the clip's 60 s are past, so that encoding its 3,000 frames (about 10 s on 4 threads) doesn't run beside the grabs while the Lie Detector is up (the user asked, 2026-10-25; it was written as the player's recording began). What the ring held at the alarm is held meanwhile (about 0.45 GB more for that minute, as the ring lets its oldest go). Before it's written, the Lie Detector's recordings are pruned to their 10 GB, the parts before counted (The Lie Detector's recordings: Disk). Its summary has the alarm and `alarm_t`, the alarm's moment in it (ALARM.json's time of writing). With no recording of the player's begun within 20 s of the alarm (the player not running, or `watch --lie-detector` raised it), the part goes to `lab/recent/<time>-lie-detector[-<form>]/` (`-quiz-warning`: the alarm's `form`, when it says one). An alarm already up when the recorder starts isn't saved (it holds nothing yet).
- **Each alarm's own clip** (since 2026-10-25, the user: "a 2nd line of defense"): from 10 s before the alarm to 60 s after (`CLIP_BEFORE_S`, `CLIP_AFTER_S`; the alarm's moment as for `before/`, ALARM.json's time of writing), 10 a second, in `lab/alarm_clips/<time>-lie-detector[-<form>]/`, whatever becomes of the player's recording and of `before/`: none needed, none waited for. It's written once its last moment is past, about 61 s after the alarm; a recorder that stops before then writes what it has (`cut_short` true in its summary). Its moments are held by the clip as they come, so a ring that loses them first (its cap, at a bigger window) loses none of the clip's. Lossless: PNG (`00000.png` ...; `format` "png" in its summary), each frame exactly as grabbed. Its summary has `kind` "the alarm's clip", the `alarm`, `alarm_t` (10 s, the alarm's moment in it), `asked` [-10, 60] and `cut_short`. About 560 MB a clip at 1366x768, written in about 14 s, then the part before it; the oldest go past 30 GB (`CLIPS_KEEP_BYTES`, about 55; 5 GB until the user asked for 30, making room on D:, which had 27 GB free), counted by `lab-usage`. A recorder started since then is needed (the babysitter starts it again within 5 s of its process ending).
- **A saved part**: `00000.jpg` ...; `index.jsonl`, a line a frame (`i`, `file`, `t` s from the part's first moment, `at` the clock time) and a line a gap (the moments in a row with no frame for one reason: `gap`, `t` and `until`, `ticks`); `summary.json` (`kind`, `from`, `to`, `seconds`, `ticks`, `frames`, `bytes`, the `gaps` in s by why, `fps`, `jpeg_quality`, the `window`'s size).
- **To look at one**, `sheet FOLDER`: a contact sheet of a frame a second, a quarter size, labelled with the clock time (`sheet.png` in the folder; `--every`, `--shrink`, `--start` and `--end` in s; 2 s for a minute's). `sheet FOLDER --video`: an mp4 playing in real time (`video.mp4`), a gap a grey card saying why, the clock time at the top left. It's OpenCV's own MPEG-4 Part 2 (its build has no H.264): about 30 dB, the HUD's text legible; a minute of the saved frames was 10 MB and took 18 s to write (`video_bench.py`). Both read the Lie Detector's recordings as well.
- **How it's doing**: `recorder --status` prints its status file: whether the game shows, what it keeps (s, frames, MB, gaps), and over the last 5 s its frames a second, ms a frame, CPU a frame and share of a core; its saves. Its log says when the game starts and stops showing, each save, and every 10 minutes what it keeps and costs. One recorder runs at a time (a named mutex: a second says so and exits). A moment that goes wrong (a grab that fails: the desktop locked, a UAC prompt) is a gap, logged the first time and every 1,000th; nothing stops it but Ctrl+C or its process ending, and with it what it holds.
- **Where it runs**: from a checkout whose `gamelab/lab` is the player's, as the alarm, the Lie Detector's recordings and the inbox are in it: the main checkout, or a live worktree (their `lab` is the main checkout's, through a junction) that has `control/rolling.py`. `save-recent` and `recorder --status` are run from any of those.

**To check in game** (nothing it does sends a key):
- `recorder --status` says "running" and, with the game in front, "the game on screen: recording", about 10 frames a second and about 0.13 of a core; with another window in front, "not recording: not in front" (or "covered"), and the gaps count up. After 5 minutes it keeps 300 s, about 2.2 GB at 1366×768 (650 MB before the frames were kept losslessly), with nothing `trimmed`. Task Manager: the python running `gamelab recorder` at below-normal priority, about 2.3 GB (the ring's 2.2 and the rest, as the JPEG ring's 650 MB were a 712 MB process; not seen live yet).
- Put another window over the game for a few seconds, then `save-recent --seconds 30 --name check`: the folder has about 300 moments, a `covered` or `not in front` gap where the window was, and `sheet FOLDER` shows the game in every tile, nothing of the other window.
- During a hunt, its capture should stay DXGI (no "falls back to mss" in its log) and the feed's frames a second as before; the game's frame rate as before (`check` on a recording made with the recorder running). mss copies with CAPTUREBLT, which on some PCs makes the Windows pointer flicker as it copies: the game draws its own pointer, but look for it over the desktop.
- At the next Lie Detector, `lab/lie_detector/<time>/before/` appears within 20 s of the alarm, about 3,000 frames, its `alarm_t` about 300; the frames before it show what came up.

## Use

From the repo root, in the `maplehelper` conda env:

```bash
python -m gamelab player --elevate
python -m gamelab calibrate
python -m gamelab combo jump_back_shot --runs 4 --chain 8
python -m gamelab stop
```

1. `player --elevate` starts the player. Approve the UAC prompt.
2. `calibrate` finds her on screen and keeps her name tag's template. Stand her on flat, textured ground, such as a girder, with nothing around. The recordings (`--record`, `do`, `combo`, `lock`) are cropped around her name tag where it is when they start, found with that template (`calibration.crop_now`): calibrate's own crop is kept while her tag is within 40 px of where it put it, and used, with a warning, only when the tag isn't found. (On 2026-10-21 the 16 runs of a sweep of `act --record` were cropped from a calibration she had walked away from, and none had her.)
3. `combo` plays the combo, here 4 runs of 8 shots in a row, and reports every jump and throw.
4. `stop` shuts the player down.

Other commands (`python -m gamelab -h` lists them all):

| Command | What it does |
|---|---|
| `hunt look` | What she sees now: her map position, HP/MP, room to walk on her platform, her EXP number as read, the monsters in her shooting band with their platform, whether a star reaches them (or is stopped by a wall of her foothold group: "behind a wall"; by a floor, her own for one below her: "a floor in the way"; or the attack takes one first whose star is: "behind one whose star is stopped"), which way they face and the template that matched, and the drops on every platform in view (annotated `lab/hunt_look.png`: each monster's feet as a dot, with an arrow the way it faces); sends no keys |
| `hunt clear --ground 17 --kills 6` | Shoot the monsters on platform 17 (and any on hers), looting kills that fall on her platform |
| `hunt kill --near -200` | Kill one monster (the one closest to that many map units away) and loot it |
| `hunt shots` | Hits and misses of every throw logged so far, by how far above or below her the target was. `lab/shots2.jsonl` also keeps her x, the target's x, her foothold group, whether a wall of it stood in the way, every monster in front of her at the throw and at the first look after it that placed her (about when the attack picked its target, timed from the key), and the target's HP bar at every look until the throw was scored. A bar drop or a kill is a throw's only if no other throw's damage can have shown then: from 0.47 s after its key (the star's release) to 0.3 s past when it was due (with its flight), as the game may show it at either and the simulator shows it at the release. Else the throw stays undecided. `lab/shots.jsonl`, logged until 2026-10-20, made every throw in flight at a kill a hit |
| `probe list`, `probe run NAME`, `probe score` | The in-game probes of what a star hits (see Probes to run): what each settles, running one, and weighing each question's two answers on their log, `lab/probe_geometry.jsonl` (`list` and `score` send no keys) |
| `map`, `map --offline` | The map's platforms and her position on them (`lab/shots/map_<id>.png`); sends no keys |
| `nav 35`, `nav 35 --fight` | Go to platform 35; with `--fight`, shoot monsters on the way instead of avoiding them |
| `nav 17 --avoid climb` | A kind of move the route takes only if nothing else gets there (each costs 20 times as much): from 23, the two rope hops instead of the climb past its end |
| `nav 3 --ways 3`, `tour --loop --ways 3` | Each trip chooses among the 3 best distinct ways to where it's going, by a softmax over their times, not only the quickest (`route.ROUTES`; off by default: in the simulator as much EXP, and fewer of her drops picked up; see Route diversity; not tried in game) |
| `tour --loop --ground-tie 0.1` | Of the grounds within 10% of the best's score, the one she left longest ago goes next (`hunt.GROUND_TIE`; off by default: nothing in the simulator measures it; see Route diversity; not tried in game) |
| `tour --stops 3 --kills 5` | A hunting sweep of the map, then rest and stand by |
| `tour --loop --minutes 20` | The hunt loop instead of the sweep's stops (`docs/hunt-modes.md`; simulator-tested only): one decision after every look, for 20 minutes, then rest and stand by |
| `tour --loop --grind` | The hunt loop going round the map in a loop of stops chosen by EXP a minute, pulled to the monsters she sees, shooting what comes into reach on the way and leaving a stop as soon as staying gives less than going on, instead of hunting one ground at a time (`behavior/grind.py`; see Grinding; simulator-tested only) |
| `patrol [FILE] [--map M]` | Grinding's view of a map (its stops, the loop it would take, camping's best), and with a recorded run, the next stop grinding would have taken every few seconds along it, and why; no game, no keys |
| `memory FILE --out OUT` | Her memory of the monsters run again on a recorded run (its looks' monsters and what each searched, her kills from its 'kill' events): what it held, and a copy of the run with its 'memory' records for `minimap --replay OUT`; no game, no keys |
| `tour --rest-avoid` | On the trips to rest and to stand by, keep clear of monsters instead of shooting the ones in the way (simulator-tested only) |
| `minimap` | The live mini-map, following the run in progress: a window beside the game, and the same as a web page for a phone on the same Wi-Fi; see below |
| `minimap --window-only`, `minimap --web-only` | Just one of the two (`web` is short for `minimap --web-only`) |
| `minimap --replay FILE`, `minimap --plan 36 2` | Replay a recorded run, or show the route between two platforms; no game needed |
| `sim SCENARIO --speed 4` | Run the stack offline on the simulator, recorded for the mini-map; `sim list` names the scenarios |
| `do walk left 150`, `do climb_rope 300`, `do pick_up 2`, `do rest 40` | Run one move from `maplehelper/actions.py` in the game, recorded |
| `act right*800 alt 500 @960,540 --record` | Ad-hoc steps: `KEY` tap, `KEY*MS` hold, `KEY+`/`KEY-` press/release, `MS` wait, `@X,Y` move the mouse |
| `combo NAME --regular-attack` | A combo test, throwing with the plain attack key (no MP) |
| `track RECORDING`, `sheet RECORDING --around-her`, `check RECORDING`, `list` | Her path through a recording; a zoomed contact sheet; whether keys went out on time and the game kept a steady frame rate; saved recordings |
| `sheet FOLDER`, `sheet FOLDER --video` | A recording kept as JPEG frames (`save-recent`'s, the part before a Lie Detector, a Lie Detector's): a contact sheet of a frame a second, or an mp4 playing in real time, its gaps shown (see The rolling recording); no game |
| `lock attack 700 740 780` | Whether a jump that many ms after an attack goes through |
| `snap` | Screenshot of the game in `lab/shots/snap.png` (commands save their pictures in `lab/shots`: the lab's own screenshots are test data) |
| `spot`, `spot --image FILE` | Whether the Lie Detector is on screen now (or in a screenshot); sends no keys |
| `bench-look [FILES]`, `bench-look --live --n 100` | Each stage of a look timed (median, p90, fastest): on saved screenshots, 1920×1080 or 1366×768 (by default 17 1920×1080 ones in `lab`; `--shrink 1366x768` times them shrunk to the game's own size, `--look-scale 1` or `2` with full- or half-size looks), or on the game as it is on screen (`--capture dxgi` or `mss`: how; by default as config.toml says). Also counted: looks that didn't find her name tag near where it was, and ropes on the whole screen that the search where the map's ropes can show missed. Live it refuses unless the game is in front already, and never brings it forward; sends no keys |
| `bodies`, `bodies FILES --recordings NAMES`, `bodies --tracks FILES` | Where the monster detector puts monsters, offline: their feet against the surface of the platform each is put on, on saved screenshots where she stands (by default bench-look's), by the matched template's origin and by its bottom; and on recordings with the camera still (by default `stand_snail` and `walk_snail`), how far their x strays from frame to frame, whether they face the way they move, how often their pose (the matched template's frame) is what they do, walking or standing, by cut-outs and sprites, and how fast each kind walks. `--tracks` reads `watch`'s telemetry instead: each track's velocity against how far its x went by the next record, for those standing and those walking. No game, no keys |
| `heading FRAMES`, `heading --learn left FRAMES`, `heading --seed --fresh --learn left FRAMES` | Which way she faces on saved frames (her picture above her name tag against her pictures: see Her heading), and with `--learn`, frames whose facing you know teach her pictures (`lab/heading_1366x768.npz`; with `--seed`, the seed in `gamelab/assets` that stands in until she has her own; `--fresh` drops them first; `--tags TAGS` takes her tag where a study put it, not where a look finds it). Offline: no game, no keys |
| `bench-paste`, `bench-paste FILES --map 10004021` | A map's monsters (each kind with a colour gate) and its drops (Perception: drops of each map), the game's own pictures drawn onto frames of the game at its own 1366×768 as the game draws them (alpha blended, at known places; by default the three in `tests/fixtures/native`, every frame of each kind both ways twice), looked for there and on the same scenes stretched to 1920×1080 as the window stretched them: how many the whole screen's search found and a fight's band around each, where against where they were drawn (map units), their scores, as which frame and facing, what was found that wasn't drawn (in the scenes and on the frames as they are), and how long each search took; then re-finds: each monster found, drawn where it stood as a frame it may show next (itself, its pose's next, standing to walking or back, a flinch) and looked for again as the feed does, with the fallback to the kind's whole set and without; `--full-step-1` also searches with step 1 at full size (as before the search went coarse to fine). See Screen size. No game, no keys |
| `learn-colours FRAMES --map 10002073` | A map's background colours from saved frames of it (any window size, or folders of them), for its monsters with no hand-made colour test: kept in `assets/backgrounds/<map id>.npz`, from which the map's detector learns their gates (see Perception: learned gates); offline, no keys |
| `bench-capture --n 100` | A look's capture and a glance's timed through DXGI and through mss, on the game's window as it is on screen (or the screen's top-left 1920×1080 without the game), and whether the two see the same pixels; never brings anything forward; sends no keys |
| `perceive`, `perceive --map 10002073`, `perceive --frames DIR` | The perception service (see The perception service): the game looked at every frame at all times by one process, its latest state handed to every command and published for the mini-map (`lab/telemetry/<time>-perceive.jsonl`); waits for the game window, never sends a key nor brings the game forward; one at a time on the PC (a second says so and ends); the babysitter keeps it running, and a command starts it if none runs. `--frames`: saved frames instead of the game, offline, at a pipe of its own (commands reach it with `GAMELAB_PERCEIVE=saved`); it ends itself if its frame loop hangs 10 s, for another to be started |
| `watch`, `watch --minutes 10 --lie-detector`, `watch --heading` | What the perception service sees, on the console every 10 s: its frames a second, the monsters followed, their deaths and whose, her EXP, who's attached (a viewer of it: no keys, the game never brought forward; it starts the service if none runs); with `--lie-detector` it also checks the service's frame for the Lie Detector twice a second and pages whoever is at the PC; with `--heading` it says which way she faces off every frame |
| `nav 35`, `tour --loop`, `hunt clear`, `pet --every 1` | Every look and glance is the perception service's latest state (since 2026-10-25; `--feed`/`--no-feed` and `--sweep` are gone): the command attaches as a client (the hunting commands send keys and tell her throws, `pet` sends keys; `hunt look`, `map` and `watch` only look), and at its end says what it had of it (states, times none came in time, connections) |
| `coverage tour-game loop-game --live` | How often each move of the map's routes was planned, taken and missed in saved harness runs and the live telemetry, her time on each platform, and the moves never taken with their cost gap and why they lost; a picture of the map with them (`lab/coverage_<map>.png`); with `--ways 3`, how often trips choosing among 3 ways would have gone through each move. See Route coverage. No game, no keys |
| `respawn`, `respawn FILES` | How soon after a kill that empties a platform a monster is seen there again, how long a drop lies before it's gone without her picking it up, and how full the platforms monsters spawn on were while in view, from the live telemetry (or the files given), as survival estimates over censored observations (Kaplan-Meier: n, the median and its 95% interval, the share left at 5 s to 180 s), against the map's own spawn fields in the game data. See Respawns and drop life. No game, no keys |
| `lab-usage`, `lab-usage --prune-telemetry` | What gamelab keeps under `gamelab/lab`, by kind (recordings, the Lie Detector's recordings, `save-recent`'s, the alarms' clips, screenshots, telemetry, harness runs, golden traces, the player's inbox and outbox, the rest), and the telemetry and recordings due to go; deletes nothing, unless `--prune-telemetry`: then the telemetry a run starting now would (see Memory and disk) |
| `recorder`, `recorder --status` | The rolling recording: the game's window 10 times a second, the last 5 minutes kept in memory, saved into a Lie Detector's recording at its alarm and by `save-recent` (see The rolling recording); `--status` says how the running one is doing. Reads the screen only, keeps nothing of another window, sends no keys; the babysitter keeps it running |
| `save-recent`, `save-recent --seconds 60 --name popup` | The running recorder's last 5 minutes (or 60 s) saved to `lab/recent/<time>[-name]/`; prints the folder |
| `clear-alarm` | After you've answered a Lie Detector yourself and it has been off the screen for a minute: lets gamelab run again (refused while its recording is still going, exit code 3); a running player then stops, and `player --elevate` starts it again |
| `pet`, `pet --every 1`, `pet --read` | Her pet kept fed (`behavior/pet.py`): its fullness read once off her Character Info (a double-click on her, then Esc) and followed in time, a Pet Food when it's low; `--every 1` follows it every minute until stopped, counting only time the game is there; `--read` reads it now. With the Lie Detector alarm up it goes on: foods by the fullness followed (the player takes them then, 3 a minute at most), no read until the alarm is cleared (On the Lie Detector). With `--every`, while the client is another size or the game draws off it, it waits, sending nothing (Behavior: Her pet) |

**On another map** every command that reads the map takes `--map` (by default the first, Thicket Around the Beach II, 10000012): West Rocky Mountain II is `--map 10004021`, Tree Dungeon, Forest Up North II `--map 10002073` (`hunt look --map 10002073`, `nav 8 --x 137 --map 10002073`, `tour --loop --minutes 10 --map 10002073`, `watch --map 10002073`). Offline, the simulator's scenarios carry their map (`sim nav_tree_bottom_to_top --speed 4`, `sim loop_rocky_bridge`), and the harness and the coverage report take `--map` (Offline: the simulator; In game, on each map, for what's known and what to check there first).

`hunt kill`, `hunt clear`, `nav`, `tour` and `probe run` bring the game to the front before their first look and wait up to 3 s for it to come (`__main__.FRONT_WAIT_S`); with `hunt look` and `map`, a game minimized is restored and brought forward first, before its window's size is taken (`_game_geometry`): the player refuses every plan while another window is in front, and a look captures whatever is. Windows doesn't always let a program bring another window forward; then the command says which window is in front and stops before it looks or sends anything: click on the game and run it again. (On 2026-10-21 at 01:01:30 a tour's first looks captured the Claude window for 2.5 s and its first plan was refused, "game not in front"; `window.bring_to_front` had been called and quietly done nothing. It now also switches to the game as Alt+Tab does if it hasn't come half way through the wait: `window.SWITCH_FALLBACK`, not tried in game yet.)

## The mini-map

`python -m gamelab minimap` opens a window that draws the map from the game data, and on it what the stack sees and plans, as it happens (it also serves the same as a web page for a phone: see "On a phone" below):

- **Localization:** her position (yellow; a magenta ring when placed from the minimap dot because her name tag is hidden) with the way she faces and her trail, HP and MP, and how her position was worked out (rope, edge or dot).
- **Perception:** the monsters in the last look (its band, and what perception follows off it: `Scene.swept`; until 2026-10-25 a slice of the sweep), coloured by kind, with their HP bars once hit; the monsters she remembers (rings of their kind's colour where each was seen last, fading over 20 s, broken once looks covering their place have missed them; a red cross where one was killed, a dim one where one was forgotten: Her memory of the monsters); the drops seen on the ground (yellow), and those remembered for later (hollow squares); other players where their minimap dots put them (dots ringed dark, in their markers' colours: anyone red, her party orange, a guild lavender, a buddy cyan), and how many there are under the map.
- **Prediction:** where each monster can be in the next second, as the move planner sees it (red bars).
- **Motion planning:** the planned route (cyan: arcs for jumps, arrows for climbs and drops) and the chain of moves in the running key plan (magenta: where she runs, and where each move lands).
- **Attack:** where a star reaches from her (the band either side, brighter on the side she faces, cut short at a wall of her foothold group whose outer face looks at her), the monsters the last throw aimed at (red rings), and the fight's last decision.
- **The tour:** its stops in order, the current one, the spot she shoots it from, the safe platforms (teal) and the hunting grounds (bright green). Below the map, the latest log lines.
- **The hunt loop** (`tour --loop`): the grounds visited, the current one and its spot; under the map, the rule that decided the last step, the objective, her EXP rate on this ground and the rate to beat elsewhere; on it, the piles in its drop store (hers bright, others' dim, expected ones small, urgent ones red).

**On a phone:** `minimap` also serves the same picture as a web page, for a phone on the same Wi-Fi, from the same process (no administrator rights; closing the window stops it too). `--window-only` starts just the window, and `--web-only` (or `python -m gamelab web`) just the page. If the page's port is taken, the window still opens (`--port` picks another). It prints two addresses: one for this computer, and one for the phone (opened on the computer, the page also shows a QR code of it). Each address ends in a key that changes every time, so nobody else on the network can watch; `--key WORD` keeps it the same, so a bookmark keeps working. The page shows the map, either the part around her (the default on a phone) or the whole of it, with HP and MP, the hunt loop's rule, objective and rates, the fight, the moves, her position and the latest log lines, as they happen. A replay (`--replay FILE`) gets buttons to pause, change the speed and jump 10 s (the page and the window each play it with their own controls). The first time, Windows may ask whether Python may accept connections: allow it on private networks. `--host 127.0.0.1` serves this computer only. Like the window, it only reads the telemetry: it sends no keys and never touches the game.

The server pushes the run's records to the page as they're written (Server-Sent Events, standard library only), and the page draws the map itself on a canvas, layer for layer as the window does, with the window's colours, reach and map (`/map.json`, from `render.py`). Her position is drawn 100 ms behind the run's clock, gliding between her positions (they come every 67 ms while she moves; ones more than 0.6 s apart aren't glided between), and the page follows a replay's pause and speed. It used to fetch the text every 400 ms and then a 40 KB picture of the map: 1.6 pictures a second, 280 ms old on average and up to 540 ms on this computer, showing 43% of her positions, for 45-70 KB/s. Measured offline on 2026-10-20 with the same live tour written again at its own pace (45 s, on 127.0.0.1):

- Every one of her 174 positions reached a client reading the stream (a Python script, on the same computer), 22-23 ms after it was written on average (p99 39-41 ms, at most 39-50; three runs).
- 2.0 KB/s, plus a snapshot of 3-10 KB when a page connects and the map (6 KB) once.
- The whole process (the server, the script writing the run again and the clients reading it) used 0.1-0.2% of a core, and 0.2-0.4% with three clients.
- In the desktop app's browser (Chromium, 3 device px per CSS px), the page drew at 60 frames a second, 0.2 ms a frame (p90 0.3 ms, at most 0.6), and only when something moved. Following the same run there for 60 s, the records reached the page 20 ms after they were written (p90 37 ms, at most 46, by the browser's own clock, which is this computer's), and its `?debug=1` corner said 22 ms (p90 37, at most 47).
- After the server was stopped and started again under an open page, the page had the new server's snapshot within a second of it starting.

A phone that was locked, or had the page in the background, catches up by itself. iOS Safari can leave a stream open but dead after the screen was locked, so the server pings every 5 s, and the page opens the stream again when nothing came for 12.5 s, or when it's shown again after more than 6 s of quiet; every connection starts with a full snapshot. Past 16 open streams the oldest ends (a phone gone from the Wi-Fi leaves one open until a write to it fails), and its page says so and waits to be shown again or reloaded, rather than come back and end the next oldest. Add `&debug=1` to the address for a corner with the frame rate, the drawing time, messages and KB a second, reconnects, how late the records come (p50 and p90 over the last 5 s; Wi-Fi adds about half the round trip shown next to it), and how long ago the newest record was written. That last isn't a delay: between moves she's looked at only a few times a second, so in five recorded live tours the newest record was over 250 ms old 30-58% of the time, with no network at all. Her position is drawn another 100 ms behind, to glide. `&delay=0` draws her where she was last seen instead of gliding. `/frame.jpg` (the map as a JPEG, `?view=her` around her) and `/state.json` (the text) are still served for a quick look, but the page doesn't use them. WebRTC was left out: the server would need aiortc and FFmpeg's libraries, for a stream of 2 KB/s on a home network. Not tried on an iPhone yet.

Hunting commands (`hunt clear`/`kill`, `nav`, `tour`) record what they see and plan to `lab/telemetry/<time>-<command>.jsonl` (`gamelab/telemetry.py`). The window follows the newest of those files, so it can be opened before or during a run. A run past 64 MB goes on in a new part, `<time>-<command>-part2.jsonl` and so on, which starts with the run's session record marked as the continuation of the part before (see Memory and disk); the window goes on into it. Live, the window never takes focus from the game: the player only presses keys while the game is in front, and screen captures see whatever is on top. So it goes beside the game window when the screen has room, and clicks on it don't activate it. A replay (`--replay FILE`, also last night's `lab/sweep_track.json`) is an ordinary window: space pauses, + and - change the speed, and the arrow keys jump 10 s.

## Offline: the simulator and golden traces

`gamelab/sim` is a small game that the whole stack runs against, with no game and no key player. `FakeEyes` answers perception's questions from a simulated world (monsters with where their feet are, which way they face and whether they walk, stand or flinch, as the templates tell; her EXP number drawn with the game's glyphs, for the stack to read), and `FakePlayer` runs key plans on it on a fake clock. Her body moves as the engine moves her (`motion/body.py`, below, which the planner's constants come from): the game sees each key a measured lag after it goes out, an arrow speeds her up and letting go brakes her, a jump gets the kick, the opposite arrow brakes her in the air, and an attack stops her landing; she climbs as measured. Monsters walk their platforms as fast as the game data says (see Localization), touch her (what a touch takes, and how often she evades it, by the monster's kind: below), are pushed back by hits, die from stars, give EXP and drop loot. An attack picks its target when it starts, about 65 ms after its key, from where she is then (as in game; see Attack): on the ground the claw stabs the nearest monster whose body its stance's box reaches, else the star goes to a monster the star's search picks, band by band; from a jump, to the nearest whose body reaches into the attack's area (with `world.AIR_PICK = 'v83'`, as from the ground, from where she is then). Each picks by the bodies, whatever the terrain; then the line to a star's pick alone is checked, and stopped, the attack hits nothing (Attack: what stops a star). The bodies are as tall as the game data has them walking (taller for 0.6 s after a hit, while they flinch). So her jump-throws go over snails as they do in game, and from the ground a star goes over a monster right under her. 86 scenarios cover navigation (every kind of move, a rope jumped at past a platform's end, two rope hops, the basin's right side up, off a rope with nothing at its top, monsters in the way, fighting on the way, cut-off plans, a walk-off and a jump onto 14 right after a kill in a world that swallows arrows pressed too soon after an attack, a climb then a step back and two jumps with the lags spread, two time-outs pinned), single moves, fights (a snail cornering her on a short platform too, a step wall between her and the monsters), kills, mop-ups, a full inventory, tours and rests (with and without the fighting escort), the hunt loop (`loop_*`: the basin, a spot off the ground, leaving an empty ground, lull loot, a rest, 8 minutes end to end, the game's errors, another player, finishing what she hurt, shooting 21 from 22, a valuable drop at once, a pile on the way), the in-game probes (`probe_*`: the window's top from 13, the step wall from 4 from the ground and jumping back, jump throws at a snail), and the continuous feed (`feed_*`: twins of five of those with her looks from a feed; see Continuous perception).

**On the other maps** (from 2026-10-24). A scenario says which map it's on (`Scenario.map_id`), and the simulator plays her as she is there (`scenarios.HER`): on the first map at level 19 (HP 642, her potions on a key), as its 86 golden traces were recorded; on West Rocky Mountain II at level 23 (HP 730) and on Tree Dungeon, Forest Up North II at level 25 (HP 774, MP 623, her stars as the HP bars read them: `world.LEVEL_25`, level 24's HP 752 until 2026-10-26), with her keys from 2026-10-23 and her pet drinking her potions. On those two a touch takes HP by meowdb's formula at DEF 75 (`world.Her.defence`; a share of PADamage on the first map, as fitted), a kind the first map hasn't takes as many stars as its HP and defence need (`world.hits_of`), and a hit pushes a monster back only from its `pushed` damage in the game data (`world.PUSH_BY_DAMAGE`; any damage pushes the first map's four). What isn't known of the game there the world runs either way (`world.Realism`: `sides`, the map's platforms' ends or HeavenClient's 25 units in from its footholds; `zero_walls`, the walls of foothold group 0 stopping her whatever her group; `rope_reach`, 95 or HeavenClient's 82), and other players can camp (`Realism.camps`: each kills the monsters of its kinds on the platforms it hunts soon after they show). Up held at a portal ends the run (`world.SimLeftMap`), as a fall out of the map does: on the ground or in the air, from 100 above it to 25 below and 25 either side, as HeavenClient takes one (`mapdata.PORTAL_BOX`; whether the game takes one in the air isn't known).
